Blowup dynamics for smooth equivariant solutions to energy critical Landau-Lifschitz flow
Jitao Xu, Lifeng Zhao

Abstract
In this paper, we study the energy critical 1-equivariant Landau-Lifschitz flow mapping ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} to 𝕊 2 \mathbb{S}^{2} with arbitrary given coefficients ρ 1 ∈ ℝ , ρ 2 > 0 \rho_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\,\rho_{2}>0 . We prove that there exists a codimension one smooth well-localized set of initial data arbitrarily close to the ground state which generates type-II finite-time blowup solutions, and give a precise description of the corresponding singularity formation. In our proof, both the Schrödinger part and the heat part play important roles in the construction of approximate solutions and the mixed energy/Morawetz functional. However, the blowup rate is independent of the coefficients.



1
Introduction

1.1
Setting of the problem

We consider the energy-critical Landau-Lifschitz equation

\begin{cases}u_{t}=\rho_{1}u\wedge\Delta u-\rho_{2}u\wedge(u\wedge\Delta u),\\
\left.u\right|_{t=0}=u_{0}\in\dot{H}^{1},\end{cases}\;(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\;\;u(t,x)\in\mathbb{S}^{2},

(1.1)

where the exchange constant
\rho_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and the Gilbert damping constant
\rho_{2}>0
. This equation was proposed by Landau and Lifshitz
[
25
]
in studying the dispersive theory of magnetization of ferromagnets. It describes the evolution of magnetization in classical ferromagnet, and thus the study on (
1.1
) is of fundamental importance for the understanding of nonequilibrium magnetism, see
[
1
]
for more details. If
\rho_{1}=0
, (
1.1
) reduces to the harmonic map heat flow (
1.5
), a model in nematic liquid flow
[
8
]
. If
\rho_{2}=0
, (
1.1
) becomes the conservative Schrödinger map flow (
1.6
), which is of much fundamental interest in differential geometry
[
16
]
.

For equation
(
1.1
)
, the associated Dirichlet energy is given by

E(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}dx,

(1.2)

which is dissipative along the flow

\frac{d}{dt}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}\bigg)=-2\rho_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|u\wedge\Delta u|^{2}.

(1.3)

Moreover, the energy (
1.2
) is invariant under the mixed symmetric transformations of the scaling and the rotation

u(t,x)\to u_{\lambda,O}(t,x)=Ou\bigg(\frac{t}{\lambda},\frac{x}{\lambda^{2}}\bigg),\quad(\lambda,O)\in\mathbb{R_{+}^{\ast}}\times O(3).

A remarkable feature of (
1.1
) is that smooth solutions preserve the magnitude
|u(t,x)|=1
for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, once we fix
|u_{0}|=1
initially. In particular, there is a specific class of solutions with an additional symmetry, called the
k
-equivariant maps, which take the form

u(t,x)=e^{k\theta{\rm R}}\begin{bmatrix}u_{1}(t,r)\\
u_{2}(t,r)\\
u_{3}(t,r)\end{bmatrix},\quad\mbox{with}\quad{\rm R}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix},

(1.4)

where
(r,\theta)
is the polar coordinate on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ast}
is the homotopy degree given by

k=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big(\partial_{1}u\wedge\partial_{2}u\big)\cdot u.

A typical stationary solution of (
1.1
) is the
k
-equivariant harmonic map

Q_{k}(r,\theta)=\frac{e^{k\theta{\rm R}}}{1+r^{2k}}\begin{bmatrix}2r^{k}\\
0\\
1\hskip-1.00006pt-\hskip-1.00006ptr^{2k}\end{bmatrix},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

According to the Bogomol’nyi’s factorization
[
9
]
,
Q_{k}
is the minimizers of the Dirichlet energy (
1.2
) in homotopy-
k
class with

E(Q_{k})=4\pi|k|.

In other words,
Q_{k}
is the ground state of (
1.1
). Since this paper is mainly concerned with the 1-equivariant solutions, we use the convention
Q=Q_{1}
for the 1-equivariant ground state.

1.2
Related geometric flows

In the past decades, great progress has been made on both the harmonic heat flow problem and the Schrödinger map problem. For the harmonic heat flow,

\mbox{(Heat flow)}\quad\begin{cases}u_{t}=\Delta u+|\nabla u|^{2}u,\\
\left.u\right|_{t=0}=u_{0},\end{cases}\;(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad u(t,x)\in\mathbb{S}^{2},

(1.5)

we refer to

[
11
,
19
,
29
,
14
]
for existence and uniqueness results. Since 2-dimensional heat flow is energy critical, singularity formation by energy concentration is possible. It is known that concentration implies the bubbling off of a nontrivial harmonic map at a finite number of blowup points, see Struwe
[
42
]
, Ding and Tian
[
17
]
, Qing and Tian
[
39
]
, Topping
[
44
]
. For
k
-equivariant case, blowup near
Q_{k}
for
k\geq 3
has been ruled out in
[
23
]
, where the harmonic map is proved to be asymptotically stable. Chang, Ding and Ye
[
12
]
found the first example of the finite-time blowup solutions of heat flow. For
\mathbb{D}^{2}
initial manifold and
\mathbb{S}^{2}
target, van den Berg, Hulshof, and King
[
6
]
implemented a formal analysis based on the matched asymptotics techniques and predicted the existence of blow-up solutions with quantized rates

\lambda_{L}(t)\approx\frac{C|T-t|^{L}}{\left|\log(T-t)\right|^{\frac{2L}{2L-1}}},\quad L\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.

For
\mathbb{R}^{2}
initial manifold, Raphaël and Schweyer
[
36
,
37
]
exhibited a set of initial data arbitrarily close to the least energy harmonic map in the energy-critical topology such that the corresponding solutions blow up in finite time with the quantized blow-up rate
\lambda_{L}(t)
for any
L\geq 1
. The case
L=1
corresponds to the stable regime. Without equivariant assumption, Davila, del Pino and Wei
[
15
]
constructed a solution which blows up precisely at finite number of given points if the starting manifold is a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The profile around each point is close to an asymptotically singular scaling of a 1-corotational harmonic map with blowup rate
\lambda_{L}(t)
,
L=1
. This rate was expected to be generic for 1-corotational heat flow, see
[
6
]
. For more results on harmonic map heat flow, see
[
30
]
and the references therein.

For the Schrödinger map problem

\mbox{(Schr{\"{o}}dinger map)}\quad\begin{cases}u_{t}=u\wedge\Delta u,\\
\left.u\right|_{t=0}=u_{0},\end{cases}\;(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad u(t,x)\in\mathbb{S}^{2},

(1.6)

there are quite many works. The local well-posedness of Schrödinger map was established in
[
43
,
18
,
32
]
. When the target is
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, the global well-posedness for small data in critical spaces was proved by Bejenaru, Ionescu, Kenig and Tataru
[
3
]
. Their result was extended to the target of Kähler manifolds by Li
[
26
,
27
]
. The stationary solutions of Schrödinger flow are harmonic maps. When the energy is less than
4\pi
, 1-equivariant solutions are global in time and scatter, see
[
4
]
. Gustafson, Kang, Tsai, Nakanishi
[
21
,
22
,
23
]
showed that harmonic maps are asymptotically stable in high equivariant classes (
k\leq 3
), which precludes the blowup solutions near the harmonic maps in those cases. However, in 1-equivariant class, Bejenaru and Tataru
[
5
]
showed that harmonic maps are stable under smooth well-localized perturbations, but unstable under
\dot{H}^{1}
topology. Merle, Raphaël and Rodnianski
[
34
]
proved the existence of a codimension one set of smooth well-localized initial data arbitrarily close to
Q
which generates type-II blowup solutions, and they figured out the detailed asymptotic behavior of these solutions near the blowup time. Perelman
[
38
]
presented another type-II blowup solution with different singularity formation.

There are a lot of works devoted to the study of Landau-Lifshitz flow. The global existence of weak solutions or partial regular solutions have been shown in
[
2
,
10
,
20
,
24
,
33
,
45
]
. However, the dynamical behavior is much less studied. The asymptotic stability of ground state harmonic maps in high equivariant classes were proved by Gustafson, Nakanishi and Tsai
[
23
]
. In 1-equivariant class, the solutions with energy less than
4\pi
were proved to converge to a constant map in energy space by Li and Zhao
[
28
]
. The equivariant blowup solution was constructed in
[
7
]
by formal asymptotics and was verified by numerical experiments. However, as the authors said in
[
7
]
‘
mathematically rigorous justification is required
’. As far as we know, the problem of blowup dynamics of the Landau-Lifschitz equation near
Q
remains open until now.

1.3
Statement of the result

Our work is in continuation of the investigation of the Schrödinger map
[
34
]
, the wave map
[
35
]
, the harmonic heat flow
[
37
]
. We establish the existence of smooth 1-equivariant type-II blowup solutions to (
1.1
), and gives a sharp description on the asymptotic behavior on its singularity formation.

Theorem 1.1
(Existence and description of blowup LL flow)

There exists a set of smooth well-localized 1-equivariant initial data with its elements arbitrarily close to the ground state
Q
in
\dot{H}^{1}
topology, such that the corresponding solution to (
1.1
) blows up in finite time. The singularity formation corresponds to the concentration of the universal bubble of energy in the scale invariant energy space:

u-e^{\varTheta(t){\rm R}}Q\left(\frac{x}{\lambda(t)}\right)\to u^{\ast}\in\dot{H}^{1}\;\;\mbox{as}\;\;t\to T,

(1.7)

for geometrical parameters
\big(\varTheta(t),\lambda(t)\big)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}\big(\hskip 1.00006pt[\hskip 1.00006pt0,T),\mathbb{R}\hskip-1.00006pt\times\hskip-1.00006pt\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast}\big)
with their asymptotic behaviors near the blowup time
T
given by

\displaystyle\lambda(t)=C(u_{0})\big(1+o(1)\big)\frac{(T-t)}{\left|\log(T-t)\right|^{2}},\;\;C(u_{0})>0,

(1.8)

\displaystyle\varTheta(t)\to\varTheta(u_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\;\;\mbox{as}\;\;t\to T.

(1.9)

Moreover, there holds the propagation of regularity:

\Delta u^{\ast}\in L^{2}.

(1.10)

Comments on the result

1.
On the blowup asymptotics
: The overall blowup behavior is similar to that of Schrödinger map problem
[
34
]
. One of the major distinctions is that, due to the additional heat flow term (also referred to as the Lifscthiz disspation), the approximate solution is completely different from that of Schrödinger map problem. It behaves like a combination of the approximate solution of (
1.5
) and (
1.6
). However, the appearance of the heat flow term does not deteriorate the error estimate.

2.
On the Morawetz estimate
: As in
[
34
]
, a unsigned quadratic term induced by the commutator
\big[\partial_{t},{\it\mathbb{H}}\big]
appears in the plain energy identity. It can not be controlled via the energy bounds directly (see (
5.12
)), and thus requires an extra Morawetz functional to create cancellation. The construction of a suitable functional is the core of the analysis. However, the presence of the heat flow term make our situation very complicated, which is the main difficulty of this work. The key observation we have made is the intrinsic structure of the operator
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{A^{\ast}}
gathered in Lemma
5.3
, which enables us to capture the competion of the Schrödinger part and the heat flow part, and formulate the uncontrollable term. In our construction, the ratio of
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
also plays a crucial role, based on which the exquisitely-designed coefficients (
5.20
) are eventually responsible for the distinct controls of various errors coming from the Morawetz estimate (
5.22
).

3.
On the universality of the blowup rate
: The blowup rate (
1.8
) is independent of the coefficients
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
, in spite of the evident influence of the latter. In fact, the subdued contribution of the coefficients has been observed by van den Berg and Williams through formal asymptotics
[
7
]
. Indeed, we shall see in Lemma
3.1
that the coupling coefficients involving
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
in front of the approximate profiles are consistent with the expressions in the flux computation (
3.15
). This correspondence produces cancellations in computing the modulation equations, which gives the identical dynamic system for the modulation parameters (
4.34
) as in
[
34
,
36
]
. That explains, to some extend, the reason why the blowup rate is irrelevant to the coefficients.

4.
On the codimension one instability
: In our construction, the initial data of the blowup solutions are characterized by specifying the modulation parameters
a,b
and the radiation term
w
. The choice of
b_{0}
and
w_{0}
resist small perturbations, while
a_{0}
, representing the time derivative of the phase, is unstable and thus selected accordingly afterward. as shown in Section
4
. This regime ensures, in some weak sense, that the solutions evolve from a codimension one set of smooth initial data will blow up in the way we describe in Theorem
1.1
.

Notions

We use the polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
and
(y,\theta)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where by the anticipated scaling transform, we will set
y=r/\lambda
. We use the convention

\partial_{\tau}=\frac{1}{y}\partial_{\theta},\quad\varLambda f=y\cdot\nabla_{y}f.

For any given parameter
b
, we introduce the scales

B_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}},\quad B_{1}=\frac{\left|\log b\right|}{\sqrt{b}}.

(1.11)

The cut-off function
\chi
is a smooth radially symmetric function defined by

\chi(x)=\begin{cases}1,\quad|x|\leq 1,\\
0,\quad|x|\geq 2,\end{cases}

with its scaling given by

\chi_{M}(x)=\chi\Big(\frac{x}{M}\Big),

provided any large constant
M>0
.

1.4
Strategy of the proof

Let us briefly sketch our approach for proving the Theorem
1.1
, which follows from the strategy developed in
[
34
,
35
,
36
]
.

Step 1
.
Renormalization
. We look for the 1-equivariant solution with its energy slightly higher than the ground state
Q
which takes the form

u(t)=e^{\varTheta(t){\rm R}}(Q+\hat{v})\left(t,\frac{r}{\lambda(t)}\right),

(1.12)

where
\hat{v}
is a perturbation with small enough Sobolev norm

\|\hat{v}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}\ll 1.

The blowup mechanism suggests
\lambda\to 0
as
t\to T
, and thus it is suitable to consider the renormalized function
v(s,y)
under the self-similar transformation

u(t)=e^{\varTheta{\rm R}}v(s,y),\quad\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)^{2}},\quad\frac{y}{r}=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}.

(1.13)

Then from (
1.1
) we obtain the renormalized equation for
v
:

\partial_{s}v-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda v+\varTheta_{s}{\rm R}v=\rho_{1}v\wedge v-\rho_{2}v\wedge(v\wedge\Delta v).

To understand the solution’s behavior in the vicinity of the ground state, we apply the Frenet basis associated to
Q
, that is
[e_{r},e_{\tau},Q]
with

e_{r}=\frac{\partial_{y}Q}{|\partial_{y}Q|},\quad e_{\tau}=\frac{\partial_{\tau}Q}{|\partial_{\tau}Q|},\quad Q(y,\theta)=e^{\theta{\rm R}}\begin{bmatrix}\varLambda\phi(y)\\
0\\
Z(y)\end{bmatrix},

where
\phi,\varLambda\phi,Z
are given by (
2.2
), (
2.3
). Then replacing
v
by
Q+\hat{v}
, we reformulate (
1.1
) and encounter the following repeated linear pattern in
\hat{v}
:

\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v},

which gives rise to the linearized Hamiltonian/Schrödinger operator

H=-\Delta+\frac{V(y)}{y^{2}},\quad\mbox{with}\quad V(y)=\frac{y^{4}-6y^{2}+1}{(1+y^{2})^{2}},

and also its vectorial version
{\it\mathbb{H}}
(
2.19
), (
2.26
). Finally, we obtain the following component equations of
\hat{v}
, which is equivalent to (
1.1
). (
{\rm lot}
stands for the lower order errors)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{s}\hat{\alpha}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{\alpha}&=\,\rho_{1}H\hat{\beta}-\rho_{2}H\hat{\alpha}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\phi+{\rm lot},\\
\partial_{s}\hat{\beta}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{\beta}&=-\rho_{1}H\hat{\alpha}-\rho_{2}H\hat{\beta}-\varTheta_{s}Z\hat{\alpha}+{\rm lot},\\
\partial_{s}\hat{\gamma}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{\gamma}&=-\rho_{1}\hat{\alpha}H\hat{\beta}+\rho_{1}\hat{\beta}H\hat{\alpha}+\rho_{2}\hat{\alpha}H\hat{\alpha}+\rho_{2}\hat{\beta}H\hat{\beta}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\phi\hat{\alpha}+{\rm lot},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.14)

Under the convention
\hat{v}=[e_{r},e_{\tau},Q]\hat{w}
, (
1.14
) can be rewritten as the frequently used vectorial form

\partial_{s}\hat{w}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{w}+\varTheta_{s}Z{\rm R}\hat{w}+{\rm\hat{J}}\Big(\,\rho_{1}{\it\mathbb{H}}\hat{w}-\rho_{2}{\rm\hat{J}}{\it\mathbb{H}}\hat{w}+\hat{p}\,\varLambda\phi\,\Big)=0.

(1.15)

Step 2
.
Construction of the approximate solution
. To characterize the evolution of
\lambda_{s},\varTheta_{s}
appearing in (
1.14
), we define two more modulation parameters
a,b
, and claim the following slow modulated ansatz:

a\approx-\varTheta_{s},\quad b\approx-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda},\quad a_{s}\approx 0,\quad b_{s}\approx-(b^{2}+a^{2}).

Then we seek for the approximate solution of (
1.15
) whose leading part is

w_{0}=a\hskip 0.50003pt\varPhi_{1,0}+b\hskip 0.50003pt\varPhi_{0,1}+b^{2}\hskip 0.50003ptS_{0,2},

(1.16)

where
\varPhi_{1,0},\varPhi_{0,1}
are the first order profiles responsible for the cancellation of the expressions inside the big brace in (
1.15
), while
S_{0,2}
is the second order profile dominating the third component
\hat{\gamma}
, which is chosen to obey the constraint

\hat{\alpha}^{2}+\hat{\beta}^{2}+(1+\hat{\gamma})^{2}=1.

Moreover, we set the higher order profiles
\varPhi_{i,j}
(
3.5
) to improve the precision of the approximate solution. In general, the constructions of these profiles are mainly concerned with solving a typical type of elliptic equations

\begin{cases}H\varPhi_{i,j}=E_{i,j}(\varLambda\phi,\varPhi_{k,l}),&\mbox{for}\;\;i+j=1,\\
H\varPhi_{i,j}=E_{i,j}(\varLambda\phi,\varPhi_{k,l},S_{k,l}),&\mbox{for}\;\;2\leq i+j\leq 3,\end{cases}

(1.17)

with
k\leq i,\,l\leq j,\,k+l<i+j
. Its solution can be obtained by the method of variation of constants with the known resonance
\varLambda\phi
of the Hamiltonian
H
. The constructions of
\varPhi_{1,0},\varPhi_{0,1}
(
3.22
) are quite straightforward, while the higher order profiles
\varPhi_{i,j}
require further manipulations, owing to their fast growing tails as
y\to+\infty
induced by
T_{1}-\varLambda T_{1}
where

\left\{\begin{aligned} &T_{1}=-y\log y+y+O\bigg(\frac{(\log y)^{2}}{y}\bigg),\\
&\varLambda T_{1}=-y\log y+O\bigg(\frac{(\log y)^{2}}{y}\bigg).\end{aligned}\right.

(1.18)

To handle this, a radiation
\varSigma_{b}
(
3.29
) has been applied to modify the recursive system (
1.17
), as illustrated in (
3.34
). Furthermore, the presence of
\varSigma_{b}
functions have profound influence on the error
\varPsi_{0}
of the approximate solution, the coupling formulas derived from which eventually leads to the forthcoming modulation equations

a_{s}=-\frac{2ab}{\left|\log b\right|}+{\rm lot},\quad b_{s}=-b^{2}\bigg(1+\frac{2}{\left|\log b\right|}\bigg)+{\rm lot},

(1.19)

as well as
a=-\varTheta_{s}+{\rm lot}
,
b=-\lambda_{s}/\lambda+{\rm lot}
. These identities constitute an ODE system, which determines the asymptotics for the modulation parameters. By refined computations on the ODE system, we see given
\lambda_{0}>0
, the scaling parameter
\lambda(t)
change its sign in finite time
T
, which yields the finite-time blowup.

Step 3
.
Control of the radiation
. Based on the approximate solution
\tilde{w}_{0}=\chi_{B_{1}}w_{0}
localized at the scale
B_{1}
, we seek for an actual solution, by showing the existence of the radiation
w
in the decomposition

\hat{w}=\tilde{w}_{0}+w.

Recall (
1.12
), it remains to prove the existence of
w
in suitable Sobolev space that permits the modulation equations (
1.19
), but this requires the estimate of the form

\sum_{i=0}^{4}\int\frac{|\partial_{y}^{i}w|^{2}}{1+y^{8-2i}}\lesssim\frac{b^{4}}{\left|\log b\right|^{2}},

(1.20)

which is the key to finish the analysis. This is accomplished by the energy method together with the bootstrap argument. To be specific, we introduce the energies of
w
, namely
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{4}
, at the level of
\dot{H}^{1},\dot{H}^{2},\dot{H}^{4}
respectively, and then claim the bootstrap bounds of the energies (
4.14
)–(
4.16
) on a small initial interval. By proving that refined bounds (
4.19
)-(
4.21
) hold, we know the radiation
w
is trapped in the bootstrap regime, and then the estimate (
1.20
) follows. To show the refined bounds, we derive the following differential inequality for
\mathcal{E}_{4}
:

\frac{{\rm d}}{dt}\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{E}_{4}}{\lambda^{6}}\bigg)\leq\frac{b}{\lambda^{8}}\bigg[2\big(1-d_{2}+o(1)\big)\mathcal{E}_{4}+O\bigg(\frac{b^{4}}{\left|\log b\right|^{2}}\bigg)\bigg],

(1.21)

which is the core of our analysis. The main difficulty is the appearance of the unsigned quadratic terms emerging therefrom:

\int{\rm R}\mathbb{H_{\lambda}}W_{2}^{0}\cdot\Big[-\mathbb{H_{\lambda}^{\perp}}\big(GW^{\perp}\big)+G{\rm R}W_{2}^{0}\,\Big].

(1.22)

This term, however can not be handled using the same Morawetz estimate in
[
34
]
, because of its intrinsic connection with the coefficients
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
. Therefore we construct a new Morawetz term
\mathbf{M}(t)
using the factorization of
\mathbb{H^{\perp}}
(
2.37
), with its coefficients involving
\rho_{2}/\rho_{1}
, to cancel (
1.22
). Analysis behind this cancellation splits into two cases:
|\rho_{2}/\rho_{1}|\leq 1
and
|\rho_{2}/\rho_{1}|>1
, and in both cases the interaction of (
1.22
) and Morawetz term will not perturb (
1.19
), (
1.21
). This fact suggests the invariance of the blowup speed against different
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
. Finally, we apply (
1.21
) to close the bootstrap arguments, and the description of the blowup speed as well as the behavior of the residue term
w
are direct consequences of (
1.20
), (
1.19
), which yield Theorem
1.1
.

Structure of the paper

In Section
2
, we introduce the Frenet basis, renormalized variables and the linear Hamiltonian
H
, and convert the problem to the vectorial form.
In Section
3
, we construct an approximate solution
w_{0}
to the renormalized equation, and also its localized version
\tilde{w}_{0}
, then give estimates on corresponding error terms.
In Section
4
, we are aimed at seeking for an actual solution, so we consider the correction term
w
, set up the trapped regime, introduce the definitions of energies
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{4}
, and obtain the modulation equations.
In Section
5
, we compute the mixed energy identity/Morawetz formula, and close the bootstrap bounds.
In Section
6
, we exhibit the that the finite-time blowup, and give a precise description of the blowup speed as well as the behavior of the correction term
w
.
In Appendix
A
, we list some results from
[
34
]
on the interpolation bounds of the correction term
w
, which are frequently used in Section
5
.
In Appendix
B
, we give a proof of the Lemma
5.4
, which is also a crucial component in Section
5
.

2
1-equivariant flow in the Frenet basis

2.1
The ground state and Frenet basis

Let us introduce the geometric set up of the 1-equivariant solution
u
of the Landau-Lifschitz equation (
1.1
). Maps with values in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
will be viewed as maps into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with image parameterized by Euler angle
(\phi,\theta)
. The ground state
Q
is a harmonic map of homotopy degree
k=1
satisfying

\Delta Q=-|\nabla Q|^{2}Q.

(2.1)

Its explicit formula is

Q(r,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\sin(\phi(r))\cos(\theta)\\
\sin(\phi(r))\sin(\theta)\\
\cos(\phi(r))\end{bmatrix},\quad\mbox{with}\quad\phi(r)=2\arctan(r).

(2.2)

For the ease of notations, we define the following functions

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varLambda\phi(r)=r\partial_{r}\big(2\arctan(r)\big)=\frac{2r}{1+r^{2}}=\sin(\phi(r)),\\
&Z(r)=\frac{1-r^{2}}{1+r^{2}}=\cos(\phi(r)),\end{aligned}\right.

(2.3)

according to which (
2.2
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle Q(r,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\varLambda\phi(r)\cos(\theta)\\
\varLambda\phi(r)\sin(\theta)\\
Z(r)\end{bmatrix}=e^{\theta{\rm R}}\begin{bmatrix}\varLambda\phi(r)\\
0\\
Z(r)\end{bmatrix}.

In order to study the flow in the vicinity of
Q
,
we choose a suitable gauge, namely Frenet basis
\big[e_{r},e_{\tau},Q\big]
, with

\begin{gathered}e_{r}=\frac{\partial_{r}Q}{|\partial_{r}Q|}=e^{\theta{\rm R}}\begin{bmatrix}Z\\
0\\
-\varLambda\phi\end{bmatrix},\quad e_{\tau}=\frac{\partial_{\tau}Q}{|\partial_{\tau}Q|}=e^{\theta{\rm R}}\begin{bmatrix}\,0\,\\
\,1\,\\
\,0\,\end{bmatrix}.\end{gathered}

(2.4)

The action of derivatives and rotations in the Frenet basis are given by the following lemma. The proof follows from direct computations.

Lemma 2.1
(Derivation and rotation of Frenet basis
[
34
]
)

There holds:

(i)
Action of derivatives:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{r}e_{r}=-(1+Z)Q,\\
&\partial_{r}e_{\tau}=0,\\
&\partial_{r}Q=(1+Z)e_{r},\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\quad\;\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varLambda e_{r}=-\varLambda\phi\,Q,\\
&\varLambda e_{\tau}=0,\\
&\varLambda Q=\varLambda\phi\,e_{r},\end{aligned}\right.

(2.5)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\tau}e_{r}=\frac{Z}{r}e_{\tau},\\
&\partial_{\tau}e_{\tau}=-\frac{Z}{r}e_{r}-(1+Z)Q,\\
&\partial_{\tau}Q=(1+Z)e_{\tau},\end{aligned}\right.\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta e_{r}=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}e_{r}-\frac{2Z(1+Z)}{r}Q,\\
&\Delta e_{\tau}=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}e_{\tau},\\
&\Delta Q=-2(1+Z)^{2}Q.\end{aligned}\right.

(ii)
Action of rotations:

{\rm R}e_{r}=Ze_{\tau},\quad{\rm R}e_{\tau}=-Ze_{r}-\varLambda\phi\hskip 0.50003ptQ,\quad{\rm R}Q=\varLambda\phi\,e_{\tau}.

Moreover, the scaling and rotation symmetries yield the two parameters family of the harmonic map

Q_{\varTheta,\lambda}(r)=e^{\varTheta{\rm R}}Q\Big(\frac{r}{\lambda}\Big),\quad(\varTheta,\lambda)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast},

with the infinitesimal generators:

\frac{d}{d\lambda}(Q_{\varTheta,\lambda})\big|_{\lambda=1,\varTheta=0}=-\varLambda\phi\,e_{r},\quad\frac{d}{d\varTheta}(Q_{\varTheta,\lambda})\big|_{\lambda=1,\varTheta=0}=-\varLambda\phi\,e_{\tau}.

2.2
The component and vectorial formula

Let us introduce two time-dependent geometrical parameters
\varTheta(t),\lambda(t)
, and look for solutions of a specific form

u=\mathcal{S}(Q+\hat{v})=e^{\varTheta(t){\rm R}}(Q+\hat{v})\bigg(t,\frac{r}{\lambda(t)}\bigg),

(2.6)

where we denote
\mathcal{S}
the mixed transformation corresponding to scaling and rotation induced by
\lambda,\varTheta
, and the map
\hat{v}
is a small perturbation near
Q
. We express
\hat{v}
under component form with respect to the Frenet basis

\hat{v}=\big[e_{r},e_{\tau},Q\big]\hat{w},\quad\hat{w}=\big[\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma}\big]^{T}.

(2.7)

As the image of
u
lies in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, the component functions
\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma}
obey the constraint

\hat{\alpha}^{2}+\hat{\beta}^{2}+(1+\hat{\gamma})^{2}=1.

(2.8)

The LL map problem is equivalent to the existence of the component functions
\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma}
, coupling with the dynamical behavior of the geometrical parameters
\lambda,\varTheta
. In this subsection we derive the equation for
\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma}
and its vectorial form. We apply the commonly used self-similar transformation:

\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}(t)},\quad\frac{y}{r}=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)},

(2.9)

which actually defines a renormalized time
s
going from certain starting time
s_{0}
, eventually to
+\infty
, as the original time
t
runs from
0
to the lifespan
T
of
u
. Accordingly, for any given function
v(t,r)
, we use the convention

v_{\lambda}(t,r)=v\Big(t,\frac{r}{\lambda}\Big)=v(s,y),

(2.10)

which leads to

\partial_{r}v_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\lambda}(\partial_{r}v)_{\lambda},\quad\Delta v_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}(\Delta v)_{\lambda}.

Also, for the transformation
\mathcal{S}
, there holds

\mathcal{S}v=e^{\varTheta{\rm R}}v_{\lambda},\quad\mathcal{S}(v_{1}\wedge v_{2})=\mathcal{S}v_{1}\wedge\mathcal{S}v_{2}.

Invoking these with (
2.1
),
we compute each term in (
1.1
) for solution (
2.6
):

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\,\,u_{t}=\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}Q)+\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}\hat{v}),\\
&\,\rho_{1}u\wedge\Delta u=\frac{\rho_{1}}{\lambda^{2}}\mathcal{S}\Big[Q\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})+\hat{v}\wedge\Delta\hat{v}\Big],\\
&\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt-\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt\rho_{2}u\wedge(u\wedge\Delta u)\hskip-1.00006pt=\hskip-1.00006pt-\frac{\rho_{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\mathcal{S}\Big\{(Q+\hat{v})\wedge\Big[Q\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})+\hat{v}\wedge\Delta\hat{v}\Big]\Big\}\\
&\phantom{-\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt\rho_{2}u\wedge(u\wedge}=\frac{\rho_{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\mathcal{S}\Big\{\big(e_{r}\cdot(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\big)e_{r}+\big(e_{\tau}\cdot(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\big)e_{\tau}\\
&\phantom{\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt\rho_{2}u\wedge(u\wedge\Delta u)}\quad\quad\quad-\hat{v}\wedge\big(Q\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\big)-(Q+\hat{v})\wedge(\hat{v}\wedge\Delta\hat{v})\Big\}.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.11)

Considering any given function
v
, from (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and the definition of
\mathcal{S}
, we have

\partial_{t}(v_{\lambda})=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\Big(\partial_{s}v-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda v\Big)_{\lambda},\quad\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}v)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathcal{S}\Big(\partial_{s}v+\varTheta_{s}{\rm R}v-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda v\Big).

In particular, by Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}e_{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathcal{S}\Big(\varTheta_{s}Ze_{\tau}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\phi\hskip 0.50003ptQ\Big),

(2.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}e_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathcal{S}\Big(-\varTheta_{s}Ze_{r}-\varTheta_{s}\varLambda\phi\hskip 0.50003ptQ\Big),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\mathcal{S}\Big(\varTheta_{s}\varLambda\phi\hskip 0.50003pte_{\tau}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\phi\hskip 0.50003pte_{r}\Big).

Thus the time derivative of
\mathcal{S}\hat{v}
is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}\hat{v})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}(\hat{\alpha}_{\lambda})\mathcal{S}e_{r}+\partial_{t}(\hat{\beta}_{\lambda})\mathcal{S}e_{\tau}+\partial_{t}(\hat{\gamma}_{\lambda})\mathcal{S}Q

(2.13)

\displaystyle+\hat{\alpha}_{\lambda}\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}e_{r})+\hat{\beta}_{\lambda}\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}e_{\tau})+\hat{\gamma}_{\lambda}\partial_{t}(\mathcal{S}Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\bigg\{\bigg[\partial_{s}\hat{\alpha}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{\alpha}-\varTheta_{s}\hat{\beta}Z-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\hat{\gamma}\varLambda\phi\bigg]_{\lambda}\mathcal{S}e_{r}

\displaystyle+\bigg[\partial_{s}\hat{\beta}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{\beta}+\varTheta_{s}\hat{\alpha}Z+\varTheta_{s}\hat{\gamma}\varLambda\phi\bigg]_{\lambda}\mathcal{S}e_{\tau}

\displaystyle+\bigg[\partial_{s}\hat{\gamma}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{\gamma}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\hat{\alpha}\varLambda\phi-\varTheta_{s}\hat{\beta}\varLambda\phi\bigg]_{\lambda}\mathcal{S}Q\bigg\}.

Next, the linear terms in the forthcoming
\hat{v}
equation, as exhibited in (
2.11
), are

\displaystyle\rho_{1}Q\wedge\big(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v}\big)

(2.14)

\displaystyle+\rho_{2}\Big[\Big(e_{r}\cdot(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big)e_{r}+\Big(e_{\tau}\cdot(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big)e_{\tau}\Big].

Similarly, by
\hat{v}\wedge\hat{v}=0
, the nonlinear terms in (
2.11
) is reformulated to

\begin{split}&\rho_{1}\hat{v}\wedge\Delta\hat{v}-\rho_{2}\hat{v}\wedge\Big(Q\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big)-\rho_{2}(Q+\hat{v})\wedge(\hat{v}\wedge\Delta\hat{v})\\
=&\,\rho_{1}\hat{v}\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})-\rho_{2}\hat{v}\wedge\Big(Q\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big)\\
&-\rho_{2}(Q+\hat{v})\wedge\Big(\hat{v}\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big).\end{split}

(2.15)

Notice here there are repeated linear patterns on
\hat{v}
, namely
\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v}
, in both (
2.14
) and (
2.15
). From the property of the ground state (
2.1
), there holds
|\nabla Q|^{2}=-\Delta Q/Q=2(1+Z)^{2}
. Using the derivation of the Frenet basis (
2.5
), we compute:

\displaystyle\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v}

(2.16)

\displaystyle=\Delta\hat{\alpha}\hskip 0.50003pte_{r}+2\partial_{r}\hat{\alpha}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{r}e_{r}+\hat{\alpha}\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta e_{r}+\Delta\hat{\beta}\hskip 0.50003pte_{\tau}+2\partial_{r}\hat{\beta}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{r}e_{\tau}+\hat{\beta}\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta e_{\tau}

\displaystyle+\Delta\hat{\gamma}\hskip 0.50003ptQ+2\partial_{r}\hat{\gamma}\hskip 0.50003pt\partial_{r}Q+\hat{\gamma}\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta Q+2(1+Z)^{2}\big(\hat{\alpha}\hskip 0.50003pte_{r}+\hat{\beta}\hskip 0.50003pte_{\tau}+\hat{\gamma}\hskip 0.50003ptQ\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\hskip-1.00006pt\Delta\hat{\alpha}\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006pt\bigg(2(1\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006ptZ)^{2}\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt-\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg)\hat{\alpha}\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006pt2(1\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006ptZ)\partial_{r}\hat{\gamma}\bigg\}\hskip 0.50003pte_{r}+\bigg\{\Delta\hat{\beta}\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006pt\bigg(2(1\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006ptZ)^{2}\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt-\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg)\hat{\beta}\bigg\}\hskip 0.50003pte_{\tau}

\displaystyle+\bigg\{\Delta\hat{\gamma}-2(1\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006ptZ)\partial_{r}\hat{\alpha}-\frac{2Z(1\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006ptZ)}{r}\hat{\alpha}\bigg\}\hskip 0.50003ptQ.

The first and second components on the RHS of (
2.16
) inspire us to define the Hamiltonian/Schrödinger operator

H=-\Delta+\frac{V(r)}{r^{2}},

(2.17)

where the potential is

V(r)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}-2(1+Z)^{2}=\varLambda Z+Z^{2}=\frac{r^{4}-6r^{2}+1}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}.

(2.18)

To make the formulas brief, we further introduce the following operators, each of which maps the vector
\hat{w}
to a scalar function of its components:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \,{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})=&\,H\hat{\alpha}-2(1+Z)\partial_{r}\hat{\gamma},\\
{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})=&\,H\hat{\beta},\\
{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})=&\,-\Delta\hat{\gamma}+2(1+Z)\partial_{r}\hat{\alpha}+\tfrac{2Z(1+Z)}{r}\hat{\alpha}.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.19)

Then (
2.16
) is actually

\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v}=-\Big({\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\,e_{r}+{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\,e_{\tau}+{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\,Q\Big),

Using (
2.16
), (
2.17
) to treat (
2.14
), we have

\displaystyle\rho_{1}Q\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})

(2.20)

\displaystyle+\rho_{2}\Big[\Big(e_{r}\cdot(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big)e_{r}+\Big(e_{\tau}\cdot(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big)e_{\tau}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\rho_{1}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\Big)\,e_{r}+\Big(-\rho_{1}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\Big)\,e_{\tau}.

Finally the nonlinear terms (
2.15
) are

\displaystyle\rho_{1}\hat{v}\wedge\Delta\hat{v}-\rho_{2}\hat{v}\wedge\Big(Q\wedge(\Delta\hat{v}+|\nabla Q|^{2}\hat{v})\Big)-\rho_{2}(Q+\hat{v})\wedge(\hat{v}\wedge\Delta\hat{v})

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\rho_{1}\Big(\hat{\gamma}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\Big)

\displaystyle+\rho_{2}\bigg(-\big(\hat{\beta}^{2}+\hat{\gamma}^{2}+2\hat{\gamma}\big){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\hat{\alpha}\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\hat{\alpha}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\bigg)\bigg\}\,e_{r}

\displaystyle+\bigg\{\rho_{1}\Big(\hat{\alpha}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\hat{\gamma}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\Big)

(2.21)

\displaystyle+\rho_{2}\bigg(-\big(\hat{\alpha}^{2}+\hat{\gamma}^{2}+2\hat{\gamma}\big){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\hat{\alpha}\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\hat{\beta}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\bigg)\bigg\}\,e_{\tau}

\displaystyle+\bigg\{\rho_{1}\Big(\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\hat{\alpha}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\Big)

\displaystyle+\rho_{2}\bigg(-\big(\hat{\alpha}^{2}+\hat{\beta}^{2}\big){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\hat{\alpha}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\hat{\beta}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\bigg)\bigg\}\,Q.

Inserting (
2.12
), (
2.13
), (
2.20
), (
2.21
) into (
2.11
),
and projecting them onto
\big\{\mathcal{S}e_{r},\mathcal{S}e_{\tau},\mathcal{S}Q\big\}
, we obtain the component equations of
\hat{v}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\hat{\alpha}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\varLambda\hat{\alpha}=\,\rho_{1}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}(1+\hat{\gamma})^{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}(1+\hat{\gamma})\varLambda\phi+\varTheta_{s}\hat{\beta}Z

(2.22)

\displaystyle-\rho_{1}\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\rho_{2}\hat{\alpha}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}\hat{\beta}^{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\rho_{2}\hat{\alpha}\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w}),

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\hat{\beta}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\varLambda\hat{\beta}=\,-\rho_{1}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}(1+\hat{\gamma})^{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\varTheta_{s}\hat{\alpha}Z-\varTheta_{s}(1+\gamma)\varLambda\phi

(2.23)

\displaystyle+\rho_{1}\hat{\alpha}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\rho_{2}\hat{\beta}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}\hat{\alpha}^{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\rho_{2}\hat{\alpha}\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w}),

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\hat{\gamma}

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{\gamma}=\,\rho_{1}\hat{\beta}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{1}\hat{\alpha}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\hat{\alpha}\varLambda\phi+\varTheta_{s}\hat{\beta}\varLambda\phi

(2.24)

\displaystyle+\rho_{2}\hat{\alpha}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})+\rho_{2}\hat{\beta}(1+\hat{\gamma}){\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}\hat{\alpha}^{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})-\rho_{2}\hat{\beta}^{2}{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w}).

These component equations, especially (
2.22
), (
2.23
), reveal the structure of a combination of the quasilinear Schrödinger equation and the quasilinear heat equation.

Remark 2.1

(i)
The linear term on the RHS of (
2.24
) is comparatively small owing to the presence of
\lambda_{s}/\lambda
and
\varTheta_{s}
, which eventually implies the smallness of
\hat{\gamma}
. This is actually reasonable in view of the constraint (
2.8
), and it will be confirmed in Section
3
.

(ii)
The spatial variable used here is
y=r/\lambda
instead of
r
, due to the action of
\mathcal{S}
in (
2.11
). Accordingly, modifications should be made by replacing
r
with
y
in the definitions of (
2.17
), (
2.19
), for example,
H_{y}=-\Delta_{y}+V(y)/y^{2}
. However, since the potential risk of ambiguity is low, we will still use the original notations.

An essential feature of our analysis is to keep track of the geometric structure of (
1.1
), so we rewrite the system into vectorial form. We set
e_{z}=[0,0,1]^{T}
, and define the rotation related to
\hat{w}
:

{\rm\hat{J}}=(e_{z}+\hat{w})\wedge,

(2.25)

where we note that
e_{z}\wedge={\rm R}
is just the usual rotation (
1.4
). The operators defined in (
2.19
) actually yield the vectorial Hamiltonian/ Schrödinger operator:

{\it\mathbb{H}}\hat{w}=\begin{bmatrix}\,{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\,\\
{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\\
{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}(\hat{w})\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}H\hat{\alpha}\\
H\hat{\beta}\\
-\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-1.00006pt\Delta\hat{\gamma}\end{bmatrix}+2\big(1+Z\big)\hskip-1.00006pt\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{y}\hat{\gamma}\\
0\\
\partial_{y}\hat{\alpha}+\frac{Z}{y}\hat{\alpha}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.26)

Moreover, we define the following vector

\hat{p}=\begin{bmatrix}\varTheta_{s}\\
\lambda_{s}/\lambda\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.27)

Therefore (
2.22
)–(
2.24
) can be rewritten as the following vectorial form

\partial_{s}\hat{w}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda\hat{w}+\varTheta_{s}Z{\rm R}\hat{w}+{\rm\hat{J}}\Big(\,\rho_{1}{\it\mathbb{H}}\hat{w}-\rho_{2}{\rm\hat{J}}{\it\mathbb{H}}\hat{w}+\hat{p}\,\varLambda\phi\,\Big)=0.

(2.28)

2.3
The linearized Hamiltonian

The linearized Hamiltonian/Schrödinger operator
H
(
2.17
) and
{\it\mathbb{H}}
(
2.26
) naturally arise when computing the Landau-Lifschtiz flow near
\mathcal{S}Q
(
2.16
). In this subsection, we collect some of their properties, which will be of essential importance in the derivation of Laypnouv mononicity.

The linearized Hamiltonian
H
admits the following factorization:

H=A^{\ast}A,

(2.29)

with

A=-\partial_{y}+\frac{Z}{y},\quad A^{\ast}=\partial_{y}+\frac{1+Z}{y},

where
A^{\ast}
is the adjoint of
A
. Given any radially symmetric function
f(y)
, these operators can be reformulated as

Af=-\varLambda\phi\,\partial_{y}\bigg(\frac{f}{\varLambda\phi}\bigg),\quad A^{\ast}f=\frac{1}{y\varLambda\phi}\,\partial_{y}\big(y\varLambda\phi f\big).

(2.30)

Thus, their kernels on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast}
are explicit:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &Af=0&{\rm iff}\quad&f\in{\rm span}(\varLambda\phi),\\
&A^{\ast}f=0&{\rm iff}\quad&f\in{\rm span}\bigg(\frac{1}{y\varLambda\phi}\bigg).\end{aligned}\right.

Hence considering
Hf=0
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast}
, we deduce either
f=C\varLambda\phi
or
Af=\frac{C}{y\varLambda\phi}
for any constant
C\in\mathbb{R}
. In other words, we have

Hf=0\quad{\rm iff}\quad f\in{\rm span}(\varLambda\phi,\varGamma),

where
\varLambda\phi
is a resonance of
H
at the origin induced by energy critical scaling invariance, and

\varGamma(y)=\varLambda\phi(y)\int_{1}^{y}\frac{dx}{x\left(\varLambda\phi(x)\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{4(1+y^{2})}\Big(y^{3}+4y\log y-\frac{1}{y}\Big).

The asymptotics for
\varLambda\phi,\varGamma
are

\varLambda\phi(y)=\begin{cases}2y+O(y^{3})&\mbox{as}\;\;y\to 0,\\
\frac{2}{y}+O\Big(\frac{1}{y^{3}}\Big)&\mbox{as}\;\;y\to+\infty,\end{cases}

(2.31)

and

\varGamma(y)=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{4y}+O(y\log y)&\mbox{as}\;\;y\to 0,\\
\frac{y}{4}+O\Big(\frac{\log y}{y}\Big)&\mbox{as}\;\;y\to+\infty.\end{cases}

(2.32)

Owing to the Wronskian of
\varLambda\phi,\varGamma
given by

\varLambda\phi^{\prime}\varGamma-\varGamma^{\prime}\varLambda\phi=-\frac{1}{y},

the variation of constants formula yields a regular solution to the inhomogeneous equation

Hu=f,

which is given by

u(y)=\varLambda\phi(y)\int_{0}^{y}f(x)\varGamma(x)xdx-\varGamma(y)\int_{0}^{y}f(x)\varLambda\phi(x)xdx.

(2.33)

In particular, if
f=\varLambda\phi
, the solution is

T_{1}(y)=\frac{(1-y^{4})\log(1+y^{2})+2y^{4}-y^{2}-4y^{2}\int_{1}^{y}\frac{\log(1+x^{2})}{x}dx}{2y(1+y^{2})},

(2.34)

with the asymptotics

T_{1}(y)=\begin{cases}-\frac{y^{3}}{4}+O(y^{5})&\mbox{as}\;\;y\to 0,\\
-y\log y+y+O\Big(\frac{(\log y)^{2}}{y}\Big)&\mbox{as}\;\;y\to+\infty.\end{cases}

(2.35)

To deal with the evolution of the forthcoming radiation (
4.3
), we extract the dominating part of the vectorial Hamiltonian
{\it\mathbb{H}}
, which is defined by

\mathbb{H^{\perp}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\\
\gamma\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}H\alpha\\
H\beta\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.36)

The given factorization (
2.29
) implies the corresponding factorization of
\mathbb{H^{\perp}}
, which is
\mathbb{H^{\perp}}=\mathbb{A^{\ast}}\mathbb{A}
with

\mathbb{A}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\\
\gamma\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A\alpha\\
A\beta\\
0\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathbb{A^{\ast}}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\\
\beta\\
\gamma\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A^{\ast}\alpha\\
A^{\ast}\beta\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

(2.37)

Moreover, there holds the relation of
\mathbb{H^{\perp}}
with (
1.4
):

{\rm R}\mathbb{H^{\perp}}=\mathbb{H^{\perp}}{\rm R},\quad{\rm R}{\it\mathbb{H}}{\rm R}=-\mathbb{H^{\perp}}.

(2.38)

3
Construction of the approximate solution

This section is devoted to the construction of the approximate self-similar solution to the vectorial equation (
2.28
) with controllable growth in spacial variable
y
. The blowup construction heavily relys on the behavior of the modulation parameters
\lambda,\varTheta
, and also their derivatives
\lambda_{s}/\lambda,\varTheta_{s}
, which emerged in (
2.28
). To study them, we introduce two more parameters, as well as their anticipated dynamics

a\approx-\varTheta_{s},\quad b\approx-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda},\quad a_{s}\approx 0,\quad b_{s}\approx-(b^{2}+a^{2}).

(3.1)

This approximation will be justified in subsection (
4.3
). The layout of our approximate solution resembles a series expansion of
a,b
. More precisely, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
(The approximate solution)

Let
M
be a large enough universal constant, then there exists a universal small constant
b^{\ast}=b^{\ast}(M)>0
, such that for any
\mathcal{C}^{1}
maps

a,b:\,[s_{0},+\infty)\mapsto(-b^{\ast},b^{\ast})

(3.2)

with the prior bound

|a|\lesssim\frac{b}{\left|\log b\right|},

(3.3)

and the varying scale
B_{0},B_{1}
given by (
1.11
), the following holds: There exist smooth radially symmetric profiles
\varPhi_{1,0}(y),\varPhi_{0,1}(y),\varPhi_{i,j}(b,y)
and
S_{0,2}(y)\sim|T_{1}(y)|^{2}
admiting the asymptotics

\varPhi_{1,0},\,\varPhi_{0,1}=\begin{cases}O(y^{3})&\mbox{{\rm for}}\;\;y\leq 1,\\
O(y\log y)&\mbox{{\rm for}}\;\;1\leq y\leq 2B_{1},\end{cases}

(3.4)

and

|\varPhi_{i,j}|\lesssim\begin{cases}y^{2(i+j)+1}&\mbox{{\rm for}}\;\;y\leq 1,\\
\dfrac{1+y^{2(i+j)-3}}{b\left|\log b\right|}&\mbox{{\rm for}}\;\;1\leq y\leq 2B_{1},\end{cases}

(3.5)

for non-negative integers
i,j
in the range of
2\leq i+j\leq 3
, such that

w_{0}=a\hskip 0.50003pt\varPhi_{1,0}+b\hskip 0.50003pt\varPhi_{0,1}+\hskip-1.00006pt\sum_{2\leq i+j\leq 3}a^{i}b^{j}\varPhi_{i,j}+b^{2}S_{0,2}

(3.6)

is an approximate self-similar solution to (
2.28
) in the following sense. Let

{\rm Mod}+\varPsi_{0}=\partial_{s}w_{0}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\varLambda w_{0}+\varTheta_{s}Z{\rm R}w_{0}+{\rm\hat{J}}\Big(\,\rho_{1}{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}-\rho_{2}{\rm\hat{J}}{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}+\hat{p}\hskip 0.50003pt\varLambda\phi\,\Big),

(3.7)

where
{\rm Mod}
is the modulation vector given by
(
The summations are for
2\leq i+j\leq 3
)

\displaystyle{\rm Mod}(t)

\displaystyle=a_{s}\Big(\varPhi_{1,0}+\sum ia^{i-1}b^{j}\varPhi_{i,j}\Big)

(3.8)

\displaystyle+\big(b_{s}+b^{2}+a^{2}\big)\Big(\varPhi_{0,1}+2bS_{0,2}+\sum ja^{i}b^{j-1}\varPhi_{i,j}+\sum a^{i}b^{j}\partial_{b}\varPhi_{i,j}\Big)

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\Big)\Big(\varLambda\phi\big(e_{x}+O(w_{0})\big)+\varLambda w_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle+\big(\varTheta_{s}+a\big)\Big(\varLambda\phi\big(e_{y}+O(w_{0})\big)+ZRw_{0}\Big),

and
\varPsi_{0}
is the error satisfying the estimates:

(i)
Weighted bounds:

\displaystyle\int_{y\leq 2B_{1}}\frac{|\partial_{y}^{i}\varPsi_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}|^{2}+|\partial_{y}^{i}\varPsi_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}|^{2}}{y^{6-2i}}\lesssim\frac{b^{4}}{\left|\log b\right|^{2}},\quad 0\leq i\leq 3,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\int_{y\leq 2B_{1}}\frac{|\partial_{y}^{i}\varPsi_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}|^{2}}{y^{8-2i}}\lesssim\frac{b^{6}}{\left|\log b\right|^{2}},\quad 0\leq i\leq 4,

(3.11)

(3.12)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\int_{y\leq 2B_{1}}|H^{2}\varPsi_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}|^{2}+|H^{2}\varPsi_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{b^{6}}{\left|\log b\right|^{2}}.

(3.14)

be defined by (

), then

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{(H\varPsi_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}},\varPhi_{M})}{(\varLambda\phi,\varPhi_{M})}=\frac{2(\rho_{1}ab-\rho_{2}b^{2})}{(\rho_{1}^{2}+\rho_{2}^{2})\left|\log b\right|}\bigg(1+O\Big(\frac{1}{\left|\log b\right|}\Big)\bigg),\\
&\frac{(H\varPsi_{0}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}},\varPhi_{M})}{(\varLambda\phi,\varPhi_{M})}=\frac{2(\rho_{1}b^{2}+\rho_{2}ab)}{(\rho_{1}^{2}+\rho_{2}^{2})\left|\log b\right|}\bigg(1+O\Big(\frac{1}{\left|\log b\right|}\Big)\bigg).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.15)

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

We proceed the proof by first constructing the profiles
\varPhi_{1,0},\varPhi_{0,1},S_{0,2}
, deriving the modulation vector
{\rm Mod}
, and then eliminating the growing tails induced by the
\varLambda T_{1}-T_{1}
, constructing the higher order profiles
\varPhi_{i,j}
(
2\leq i+j\leq 3
), and finally estimating the error
\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}
.

Step 1
.
Construction of
\varPhi_{1,0}
,
\varPhi_{0,1}
. Setting
\hat{w}=w_{0}
in (
2.28
) gives the RHS of (
3.7
). Assuming the smallness of
a,b
, we see the expression

{\rm\hat{J}}\Big(\rho_{1}{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}-\rho_{2}{\rm\hat{J}}{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}+\hat{p}\hskip 0.50003pt\varLambda\phi\Big)

(3.16)

is the major error to be eliminated in the first place. From the definition of (
2.25
), (
2.27
), we have

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle\hat{J}}\Big(\rho_{1}{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}-\rho_{2}{\rm\hat{J}}{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}+\hat{p}\hskip 0.50003pt\varLambda\phi\Big)={\rm\hat{J}}\Bigg\{\hskip-1.00006pt(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}{\rm R})\hskip 0.50003pt{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}-\rho_{2}w_{0}\wedge{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}-\begin{bmatrix}\,a\,\\
b\\
0\end{bmatrix}\hskip-1.00006pt\varLambda\phi\Bigg\}

(3.17)

\displaystyle-\Big(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\Big)\varLambda\phi\big(e_{x}+O(w_{0})\big)+\big(\varTheta_{s}+a\big)\varLambda\phi\big(e_{y}+O(w_{0})\big).

We decompose
w_{0}
by

w_{0}=w_{0}^{1}+w_{0}^{2}+b^{2}\hskip 0.50003ptS_{0,2},\quad\mbox{with}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} &w_{0}^{1}=a\hskip 0.50003pt\varPhi_{1,0}+b\hskip 0.50003pt\varPhi_{0,1},\\
&w_{0}^{2}=\sum_{2\leq i+j\leq 3}a^{i}b^{j}\varPhi_{i,j},\end{aligned}\right.

(3.18)

and further assume the profiles are of the form

S_{0,2}=S_{0,2}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}\hskip 0.50003pte_{z},\quad\varPhi_{i,j}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}=0\;\,\mbox{for}\;\,1\leq i+j\leq 3.

(3.19)

Thus the leading profiles of
w_{0}
should be
w_{0}^{1}+b^{2}S_{0,2}
, where
w_{0}^{1}
is mainly responsible for the first and second components, while

for the third one. From these we compute:

\displaystyle\quad(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}{\rm R})\hskip 0.50003pt{\it\mathbb{H}}w_{0}

\displaystyle=(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}{\rm R})\begin{bmatrix}\,{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(w_{0}^{1})\,\\
{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(w_{0}^{1})\\
0\end{bmatrix}+(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}{\rm R})\begin{bmatrix}\,{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(w_{0}^{2})\,\\
{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(w_{0}^{2})\\

(3.20)

\displaystyle-2b^{2}(1\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006ptZ)\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{y}S_{0,2}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\big(\rho_{1}e_{x}-\rho_{2}e_{y}\big)

3.20
) into (
3.17
), we let

(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}{\rm R})\begin{bmatrix}\,{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(1)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}}}}}(w_{0}^{1})\,\\
{\it\mathbb{H}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(2)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(2)$}}}}}}(w_{0}^{1})\\

(3.21)

which yields the equations for the first-order profiles

Using (
2.34

\varPhi_{1,0}=\frac{1}{\rho_{1}^{2}+\rho_{2}^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\,\rho_{1}\,\\
\,\rho_{2}\,\\
0\end{bmatrix}T_{1},\quad\varPhi_{0,1}=\frac{1}{\rho_{1}^{2}+\rho_{2}^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}-\rho_{2}\\

Their asymptotics (

Step 2
.

. In view of the constraint (

with sufficient precision. From the decomposition (

), the profile

3.24
), we let

S_{0,2}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\displaystyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{868894}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\textstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26665pt}{\resizebox{759247}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.22499pt}{$\scriptstyle(3)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.26666pt}{\resizebox{759248}{445645}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle(3)$}}}}}}=-\frac{1}{2(\rho_{1}^{2}+\rho_{2}^{2})}(T_{1})^{2},

(3.25)

which admits the following asymptotics from (

S_{0,2}(y)=\begin{cases}O(y^{6})&\mbox{for}\;\;y\leq 1,\\
